Verification of the Release-Acquire Semantics
Parosh Abdulla, Elli Anastasiadi, Mohamed Faouzi Atig, Samuel Grahn

Abstract
The Release-Acquire ( 𝚁𝙰 \mathtt{RA} ) semantics and its variants are some of the most fundamental models of concurrent semantics for architectures, programming languages, and distributed systems. Several steps have been taken in the direction of testing such semantics, where one is interested in whether a single program execution is consistent with a memory model. The more general verification problem, i.e., checking whether all allowed program runs are consistent with a memory model, has still not been studied as much. The purpose of this work is to bridge this gap. We tackle the verification problem, where, given an implementation described as a register machine, we check if any of its runs violates the 𝚁𝙰 {\mathtt{RA}} semantics or its Strong ( 𝚂𝚁𝙰 {\mathtt{SRA}} ) and Weak ( 𝚆𝚁𝙰 {\mathtt{WRA}} ) variants. We show that verifying 𝚆𝚁𝙰 {\mathtt{WRA}} in this setup is in 𝒪 ( [ ) n 5 ] \mathcal{O}([)n^{5}] , while verifying the 𝚁𝙰 {\mathtt{RA}} and 𝚂𝚁𝙰 {\mathtt{SRA}} is in both NP- and coNP-hard, and provide a PSPACE upper bound. This both answers some fundamental questions about the complexity of these problems, but also provides insights on the expressive power of register machines as a model.

1
Introduction

Over the years, numerous consistency models have been proposed to capture the subtle concurrency semantics of hardware architectures, programming languages, and distributed systems.
The Release-Acquire (
{\mathtt{RA}}
) semantics and its variants are some of the most fundamental consistency models weaker than sequential
consistency, which are especially common and well-studied in programming languages and distributed data stores.
Such consistency models allow different processes (threads) to have different views of the order of certain memory updates and maintain a looser global consensus on all events.
This allows for much faster implementations while still providing the user an intuitive and deterministic understanding of the underlying concurrency model.

{\mathtt{RA}}
is a fragment of the C11 model
[
19
]
, obtained by restricting the threads’ write and read instructions to be release and acquire accesses, respectively.
The
{\mathtt{RA}}
model is appropriate as a rigorous foundational semantics on its own, independently of particular architectures and compilers, and it has verified compilation schemes to popular platforms such as the x86-TSO, power, and ARM architectures
[
7
,
8
,
23
]
.
Several variants of the
{\mathtt{RA}}
semantics have been proposed in the literature in recent years.
Notably, the Strong-Release-Acquires (
{\mathtt{SRA}}
) semantics
[
19
]
strengthens
{\mathtt{RA}}
by forbidding behaviors that require the re-ordering of write instructions but coincides with
{\mathtt{RA}}
for programs that do not contain write-write races.
In
[
19
]
, it is shown that
\mathtt{SRA}
captures precisely the guarantees provided by POWER compilers for programs compiled from
{\mathtt{RA}}
.
Another variant is the Weak-Release-Acquire (
\mathtt{WRA}
) semantics that has been considered as an alternative for
\mathtt{RA}
in the semantics of shared-memory concurrent programs, permitting more efficient verification frameworks such as stateless model checking
[
16
]
.

The relevance of
\mathtt{RA}
and its siblings goes beyond compilers and hardware architectures.
At the distributed systems level, they are equivalent to standard and well-studied variants of causal consistency
[
18
]
.

\mathtt{SRA}
corresponds to the causal convergence consistency semantics implemented in data stores
[
9
,
10
]

, while
\mathtt{WRA}
corresponds to the classical definition of causal consistency
[
9
]
.

One of the most fundamental computational problems for a given consistency model
{\mathtt{CM}}
is
consistency checking
.
Consistency checking comes in two flavors:
testing
and
verification

[
26
,
5
,
15
,
1
,
20
]
.
In testing, we are given the consistency model
{\mathtt{CM}}
, often described using a set of axioms, and a program run
\rho
consisting of a sequence of events.
The sequence is typically generated by an implementation, e.g., a hardware architecture, a compiler, or a distributed protocol, that is supposed to guarantee
{\mathtt{CM}}
.
The task is to check whether
\rho
satisfies
{\mathtt{CM}}
.
The verification problem is more general: we are given an
implementation
and asked to check whether
all
executions of the implementation satisfy
{\mathtt{CM}}
.

The relevance and intricacy of the
\mathtt{RA}
-like semantics have led to several recent works on consistency checking of such models.
All these works consider the
testing
problem.
The first results showed that testing consistency under the
\mathtt{RA}
semantics is of polynomial complexity
[
1
,
17
]
.
Recently, it was shown that testing consistency checking for
\mathtt{SRA}
and
\mathtt{WRA}
have also polynomial complexity
[
26
]
.
Despite the above results on testing consistency, little is known about the complexity of
verifying
consistency under the
\mathtt{RA}
semantics.
As far as we know, the problem is still poorly understood.
The goal of this work is to bridge this gap.

Contribution:

We consider the complexity of the consistency verification problem under the
\mathtt{RA}
semantics.
To state our results, we use the classical
register machine
model to describe the underlying implementation that handles memory access.
The model is an extended finite-state machine with a finite set of registers that store data values from an unbounded domain.
The machine interacts with a finite set of external threads through write (where the register machine inputs a value to a register) and read (outputting a stored value) operations performed on a finite set of variables.
Furthermore, the machine can perform internal transitions to transfer (i.e., copy) data between registers.
We do not allow data-dependent transitions, as is common in the literature for the type of architecture we are modeling.
The model is conceptually simple, providing a concise framework to state our complexity results.
At the same time, it is sufficiently robust to model relevant features needed to model cache protocols or distributed systems, such as rendezvous communication, broadcasting fences, vector clocks, broadcast communication and store buffers
[
9
,
11
]
.
Moreover, recent works use automata-like formalisms for learning models of implementations and detecting bugs
[
14
,
12
]
. Such works enhance the relevance of register machines for verifying program behaviors, such as consistency with weak memory.

Given a register machine, we consider the verification problem, i.e. that
regardless of the interacting program, it cannot produce
a
bad behavior
, i.e., store and return values in
a way that violates any one of the
{\mathtt{RA}}
-family
of models (namely,
{\mathtt{RA}}
,
{\mathtt{WRA}}
, and

{\mathtt{SRA}}
).
To do this, one must explore all possible runs of a given register machine.
The state space of the register machine is infinite (since the data domain is infinite), and the set of paths is also infinite, so the problem’s decidability is not obvious.
Here, we show the decidability for all considered models, and in the case of large complexity
also provide lower bounds. Our main contributions are:

•

We prove the verification problem for
{\mathtt{WRA}}
is in
\mathcal{O}(n^{5})
time
(Section
3.1
).

•

We prove that the verification problem for the

{\mathtt{RA}}
and
{\mathtt{SRA}}
semantics is in
\mathsf{PSPACE}
, and

•

it is both
\mathsf{NP}
,
and
\mathsf{coNP}
-hard (see Section
4
).

The main body of our technical contribution lies in determining
a way to explore only finite (and thus finitely many) runs of
the register machine.
For our harness results we provide regacrions from the boolean satisfiability
and tautology problems respectively.

2
Preliminaries

To formulate the verification problems we study, we need two formalisms that describe the platform and the consistency model.
We use register machines and execution graphs respectively.
In what follows, we will use the following notation:

•

Given a relation
R
,
{{\mathtt{dom}}}\left(R\right)
denotes its domain;
{R}^{?}
and
{R}^{+}
denote its
reflexive and transitive closures; and
{R}^{-1}

denotes its inverse.

•

Given a function
f
, we write

{f}\left[x\rightarrow y\right]
, to denote a new function
f^{\prime}
, where
f^{\prime}(x)=y
, and
f^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=f(x^{\prime})
, if
x^{\prime}\neq x
.

•

Given an expression
S
, we denote as

S(\nicefrac{{a}}{{b}})
, the expression
S
, where all occurrences of
b
have been replaced with
a
. Note that if
S
had no occurrences of
b
then
S(\nicefrac{{a}}{{b}})=S
.

•

For a set
\mathcal{S}
and an element
a
, we denote with
\mathcal{S}\oplus a
the union of
\mathcal{S}\cup a
.
Note that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}
if
a\in\mathcal{S}
.

2.1
Register Machines

(a)

A register machine
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and a run
\rho
of
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,1\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,2\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\phi,x,3\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\phi,x,2\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta,x,3\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}_{x}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}_{x}

(b)

The egraph
G
of
\rho
(see
Section

2.3
)

Figure 1
:
Register machines and operational semantics

A
register machine
, or shortly a
machine
, is an extended finite-state automaton with a finite set of registers that store data values from an unbounded domain.
The machine performs input (write) operations and output (read) operations on a finite set of variables.
Read and write operations induce external actions that
synchronize the machine with its environment, i.e., with an external program consisting of a finite set of threads that run on the machine.

Fig.

1
(left) depicts a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
with two states
q_{0}
,
q_{1}
, and two registers
a
and
b
.
The machine
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
manages two threads
\theta
and
\phi
accessing a (single) shared variable
x
.
It starts executing from the initial state
q_{0}
with the initial register values
0
.
Each transition of the machine is labeled by an
operation
.
For instance, the transition label from
q_{0}
to
q_{1}
is the write operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,a\right)}
.
Here, the machine
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
accepts a request from the thread
\theta
to write a new value on the variable
x
, upon which the machine stores the written value in the register
a
.
The machine allows the program running on it to choose the written value.
In
q_{1}
, the machine loops performing a sequence of write operations as the one above.
The label of the transition from
q_{1}
to
q_{0}
is the write operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\phi,x,b\right)}
, in which
\phi
performs a write operation, and
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
stores the written value in
b
.
In
q_{0}
, the machine accepts read requests from the threads.
The operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta,x,b\right)}
means that
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
accepts a request from the thread
\theta
to read the value of the variable
x
, upon which the machine returns the value currently stored in the register
b
.
We can explain the operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\phi,x,a\right)}
similarly.
In the general case, a register machine is meant to allow
any
kind of request (i.e., a read or write from any thread to any variable) from the environment (program) at any time, no matter what state it is in.
Such a register machine will be called
reactive
.
We note that the machine we give as an easy example in
Fig.

1
is not reactive, but we provide a reactive version of it (for completeness) in
Appendix

A
.

Definition 1
.

Assume a set
\Theta
of threads, a set
{\mathcal{V}}
of variables, and a set
\mathtt{Regs}
of registers.
We assume that the variables and the registers range over a (potentially infinite) set
\mathtt{\mathcal{D}}
of data values with the particular value
0\in\mathtt{\mathcal{D}}
.
A
register machine

{\mathcal{M}}
is a tuple
\left\langle{Q,q_{\mathtt{init}},\Delta}\right\rangle
where
Q
is the finite set of states,
q_{\mathtt{init}}\in Q
is the initial state, and
\Delta
is the finite set of transitions.
A transition is a triple of the form
\left\langle q,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathtt{o}},q^{\prime}\right\rangle
where
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
are states, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathtt{o}}
is an operation.
The operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathtt{o}}
can be in one of the following three forms:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,a\right)}
receives the value of the variable
x
from
\theta
and writes (stores) the value in register
a
.
The environment selects the written value (the program running on
{\mathcal{M}}
).

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta,x,a\right)}
reads of value of the variable
x
from the register
a
and delivers the stored value to
\theta
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a:=a^{\prime}}
copies the value stored in the register
a^{\prime}
to the register
a
.

For any register machine, it is clear we can pre-process it to omit any
unreachable states (from
Q
).
We state and prove all remaining algorithms after this pre-processing has taken place,
and thus with all remaining states being reachable
by at least some path.

2.2
Operational Semantics

We define the operational semantics of a register machine by defining
the transition system it induces, i.e., by defining the set of configurations of the machine together with a transition relation on them.
1
1

1

We use the term
transition
to refer both to the set of transitions
in the syntax of the machine (Def.
1
)
and to the transition relation on configurations. The meaning will always be clear from the context.

A
configuration

\gamma
is of the form
\left\langle q,{\mathcal{R}}\right\rangle
where
q\in Q
defines the state of the machine, and
{{\mathcal{R}}}:{\mathtt{Regs}}\rightarrow{\mathtt{\mathcal{D}}}
defines the value
{{\mathcal{R}}}\left(a\right)
of each register
a\in\mathtt{Regs}
.
The
initial configuration

\gamma_{\it init}
is the pair
\left\langle q_{\mathtt{init}},\lambda\,\mathtt{Regs}.\,0\right\rangle
, i.e., the machine
{\mathcal{M}}
starts running from a configuration where it is in its initial state and all its registers contain the value
0
.

For example, a configuration of the machine
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
from Fig.
1(a)
is a triple
\left\langle q_{,}i_{a},i_{b}\right\rangle
describing the local state, and the contents of the registers
a
and
b
.
In this example we see that a run
\rho
consists of a sequence of transitions.
The run starts from the initial configuration where
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
is in its initial state
q_{0}
, and the registers contain their initial values
0
.
When executing a transition, we use the operation of the transition to generate an
action
describing an observable interaction between
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and its environment.

Write
{\left\langle q,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,a\right)},q^{\prime}\right\rangle\in\Delta}
{\mathtt{v}\in\mathtt{\mathcal{D}}\;\;\;{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{R}}}\left[a\rightarrow\mathtt{v}\right]}
{\left\langle q,{\mathcal{R}}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,a\right)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sslash\mathtt{v}}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}{}\left\langle q^{\prime},{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}\right\rangle}

Read
{\left\langle q,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta,x,a\right)},q^{\prime}\right\rangle\in\Delta}
{{{\mathcal{R}}}\left(a\right)=\mathtt{v}}
{\left\langle q,{\mathcal{R}}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta,x,a\right)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sslash\mathtt{v}}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}{}\left\langle q^{\prime},{\mathcal{R}}\right\rangle}

Copy
{\left\langle q,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a:=a^{\prime}},q^{\prime}\right\rangle\in\Delta}
{{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{R}}}\left[a\rightarrow{{\mathcal{R}}}\left(a^{\prime}\right)\right]}
{\left\langle q,{\mathcal{R}}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\tau}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}{}\left\langle q^{\prime},{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}\right\rangle}

Figure 2
:
The semantics of a register machine’s three operations. Write and copy operations update the state of the memory
{\mathcal{R}}
, while read operations only update the state of the register machine.

We use
\Gamma
to denote the set of configurations.
A transition is of the form
\gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\alpha}}\gamma_{2}
where
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma
are configurations, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\alpha}
is an operation augmented with a concrete value to be read or written.
We define the transition relation between configurations according to the inference rules of Fig.

2
.
In the write rule, the machine executes a transition from
q
to
q^{\prime}

while processing a write operation.
The configuration changes state accordingly and updates the value of the relevant register as implied by the operation.
In the read rule, the machine processes a read operation that returns the relevant register’s value.
The machine performs a register assignment operation in the copy rule.
The operation is not visible to the external threads; hence, it is labeled by the silent event
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\tau}
.

A run
\rho
of the program is a sequence
\gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\alpha}_{1}}\gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\alpha}_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\alpha}_{n}}\gamma_{n}
of transitions,
where each
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\alpha}_{i}
is one of the operations described in Figrue
2
.
We say that
\rho
is initialized if
\gamma_{0}=\gamma_{\it init}
, i.e., the run starts from the initial configuration.
We use
{{\mathtt{Runs}}}\left({\mathcal{M}}\right)
to denote the set of all initialized
runs of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
We say that
\rho
is
differentiated
if, for any given variable
x\in{\mathcal{V}}
, the write events in
\rho
all use different values.

2.3
Execution Graphs

We will be using execution graphs to both repreresnt a run, but also to describe our
models in the classic axiomatic style
[
24
]
.

The nodes of an
execution graph
(
egraph
for short) are
events
.
Fig.
1(b)
contains an example of an egraph.
An event corresponds to an action performed by a register machine when interacting
with its environment.
The egraph edges specify different relations on the events.
In this paper, to define our consistency models,
we will work with three binary relations (
[
18
]
): (a) the

program-order relation
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
), depicted by solid edges,
totally orders the events in each thread;
(b) the
reads-from
relation (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
), depicted by dashed edges,
associates every read event with the write event it reads from;
and (c) the
coherence-order
relation (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
), depicted by dotted edges,
partially orders the writes on each variable.
Different consistency models are defined by forbidding different types of cycles in the egraph (as described in
Section

2.4
below).
We associate the runs of a register machine with egraphs.

2.3.1
Definitions

An
event

isoftheform
(
ty
,
θ
,x,
v)
where
ty
∈{
W
,
R}
isthetypeoftheevent(writeorread),
θ
∈Θ
isthethreadperformingtheevent,
x∈
V
isthevariableonwhich
θ
conductstheevent,and
v
isthevaluethatiseitherwrittenorreadfrommemory.Wedefine
⋅
type
:=
ty
,
⋅
thread
:=
θ
,
⋅
val
:=
v
,and
⋅
var
:=x
.Wewilluseaset
InitEvents
={
init^x
∣ x∈
V}
of{\it initial}writeevents,where
init^x
representsadummyeventwritingtheinitialvalue
0
to
x
.Weassumethattheinitialeventsdonotbelongtoanythreads.Weuse

todenotethesetofallevents.\par Forasetofevents

.

\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[E\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}}\right]}\,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}}
.
Fix a set
E:{\mathtt{InitEvents}}\subseteq E\subseteq\mathtt{Events}
of events.

•

A
program-order
on
E
is a relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
defined as a union
\cup_{\theta\in\Theta}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}_{\theta}}}
such
that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}_{\theta}}}
is a total order on the set of events in
\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[E\land\theta\right]}\,
.
In other words,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
totally orders all the events in
E
belonging to each thread.

•

A
reads-from
relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\subseteq\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[E\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}}\right]}\,\times\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[E\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}}\right]}\,
assigns to each read event
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
a single write event
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}
in
E
with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{var}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{var}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{val}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{val}}}}}
.
We will write,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\right]}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
to mean that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
takes its value from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}
.

•

A
partial-coherence-order
on
E
is a relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}
defined
as a
\cup_{x\in{\mathcal{V}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}_{x}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}_{x}

is a partial order on the set of write events on
x
.
We require that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{init^{x}}}
is the smallest element in the sub-relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}_{x}
.
A
total-coherence-order
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
on
\mathtt{Events}
is a coherence-order
in which the
x
-sub-relations are total.
In other words,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}=\cup_{x\in{\mathcal{V}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}_{x}}}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}_{x}}}}}
is a total order on the set of write events on
x
.
In this paper, we only use coherence-order relations that can be derived from the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
- and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
-relations.

We also define the
happens-before
relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{hb}}}}:=({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}})^{+}
.
A
partial execution graph

G
is a tuple
\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}\right\rangle
where:
(i)

E\subseteq\mathtt{Events}
is a set of events,
(ii)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
a program-order on the set
E
,
(iii)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
is a reads-from relation on
E
, and
(iv)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}
is a partial coherence-order relation on
E
.
A
total execution graph
, or simply an
execution graph
, is a partial execution graph in which the coherence-order relation is total.

For a relation
R
, an event
E
, and a thread
\theta\in\Theta
, we write
R\theta
if
R{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{}}\in\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[E\land\theta\right]}\,
.
The initial egraph is defined by
G_{\mathsf{init}}:=\left\langle{\mathtt{InitEvents}},\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle
, i.e., it only contains the initial events, and all its relations are empty.

{\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{}\left\langle E^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}^{\prime}\right\rangle}

Figure 3
:
The rules for adding events to an egraph.
A write event only causes an update to the program order relation

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
, while a read event creates also
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
edges.
For the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
update we consider only
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{{}^{\prime}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{}}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
edges are added only when necessary,
then the resulting
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
is a partial one.

If the new event
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}
is a write event performed by a thread
\theta
, we add
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}
to the set of events.
Adding a write event does not affect the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
relations.

If the new event to be added is a read event
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
, then we also need to provide a write event
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}
that already belongs to
E
from which
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
will read its value.
The events
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}
should have identical variables and values.
We modify the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
-relation in the same manner as we did for write events.
We modify the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
-relation by adding the new pair
\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}\right\rangle
indicating that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
is reading from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}
.
Finally, we update the coherence-order so that we maintain the invariant
that the latter is a modification-order as defined in
[
19
]
.
To that end, we consider
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
to be
sources and targets, and then search for write events

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}^{\prime}}}
that are
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{hb}}}}
-before
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
to connect to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}
.

2.4
Consistency Models

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta_{1},x,1\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta_{1},x,2\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta_{2},x,2\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta_{2},x,1\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}

Figure 4
:
An execution graph that contains a cycle on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{hb}}}}

A declarative memory model is formulated as a collection of constraints on execution graphs, which
determine the consistent execution graphs—the ones allowed by the model.
In this section, we will formulate the three consistency models (
{\mathtt{CM}}
) we work with.
All mentioned memory models are weaker than Sequential Consistency (SC), and allow for less restrictive memory accesses. SC requires that as soon as some value has been written in some variable, this is immediately visible to all threads.
The models we study instead allow for several threads to still view older values written in the variable, until they become “aware” of a new write on some path that “hides” the old value.

We define a consistency model
{\mathtt{CM}}
by forbidding different forms of cycles in egraphs.
All the consistency models we consider in this paper require the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{hb}}}}
-relation to be acyclic, i.e., the transitive closure of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
is a (strict) partial order.
For instance, the egraph of Fig.
4
contains a cycle on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{hb}}}}
and hence it does not satisfy any of our consistency models.
Besides this basic acyclicity condition on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{hb}}}}
, different consistency models impose additional constraints on the egraph (
[
19
]

[
18
]
).

Definition 3
.

Let
G=\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}\right\rangle
be a (possibly partial) execution graph.

•

We write
G\models{\mathtt{WRA}}
to denote that for any
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}
event in the graph, the relation
\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}}\land{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{var}}}}\right]}\,\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{hb}}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{hb}}}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}}^{-1}
is acyclic.

•

We write
G\models{\mathtt{RA}}
to denote that
{\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}_{x}\right]}\,}^{+}
is irreflexive for each variable
x\in{\mathcal{V}}
.

•

We write
G\models{\mathtt{SRA}}
to denote that the relation
{\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}\right]}\,}^{+}
is acyclic.

For a set
E
of events, a program-order relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
on
E
, and a reads-from relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
on
E
, we write
\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\right\rangle\models{\mathtt{RA}}
if there is a total coherence-order relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
on
E
such that
\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}\right\rangle\models{\mathtt{RA}}
(rsp.
{\mathtt{SRA}}
, and
{\mathtt{WRA}}
).
We write
\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\right\rangle\models{\mathtt{RA}}
if there is a reads-from relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
and a coherence-order relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
on
E
such that
\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}\right\rangle\models{\mathtt{RA}}
(rsp.
{\mathtt{SRA}}
, and
{\mathtt{WRA}}
).

Definition 4

(Memory models over runs)
.

For a run
\rho
, we write
\rho\models{\mathtt{RA}}
to denote that
{{\mathsf{mkEgraph}}}\left(\rho\right)\models{\mathtt{RA}}

(rsp.
{\mathtt{SRA}}
, and
{\mathtt{WRA}}
).

Definition 5

(Memory models over register machines)
.

For a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
, we write
{\mathcal{M}}\models{\mathtt{RA}}
if
\forall\rho\in{{\mathtt{Runs}}}\left({\mathcal{M}}\right).\;\rho\models{\mathtt{RA}}
. (rsp.
{\mathtt{SRA}}
, and
{\mathtt{WRA}}
). We will refer to the problem of determining whether a register machine satisfies these semantics as
\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Cons}}
. (rsp.
\operatorname{\mathtt{WRA-Cons}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{SRA-Cons}}
).

In other words, a register machine satisfies the
{\mathtt{RA}}
-semantics if all its runs satisfy
{\mathtt{RA}}
.
Now that we have defined the memory models we are ready to prove the following:

Lemma 6
.

Assume a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
and
\rho\in{{\mathtt{Runs}}}\left({\mathcal{M}}\right)
. Then, for
{{\mathsf{mkEgraph}}}\left(\rho\right)=\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}\right\rangle
, if
{{\mathsf{mkEgraph}}}\left(\rho\right)\models{\mathtt{RA}}
, then there exists a total coherence order
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
, such that
\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}\right\rangle\models{\mathtt{RA}}
. (rsp.
{\mathtt{SRA}}
, and
{\mathtt{WRA}}
).

The above lemma guarantees that if for all runs
\rho
of a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
,
{{\mathsf{mkEgraph}}}\left(\rho\right)\models{\mathtt{RA}}
, then
{\mathcal{M}}\models{\mathtt{RA}}
as well.
We prove this lemma for the
{\mathtt{RA}}
semantics in Appendix
B
. A similar construction works for all the memory models in this paper.

Differentiated runs:
We write
{\mathcal{M}}\models^{{\mathtt{Diff}}}{\mathtt{RA}}

if for all the differentiated runs
\rho\in{{\mathtt{Runs}}}\left({\mathcal{M}}\right).\;\rho\models{\mathtt{RA}}
. Essentially we only consider the set of differentiated runs rather the set of all runs.
Note that for differentiated runs, the transition rules of
Fig.

3
become deterministic.
In
\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Diff-Cons}}
, the task is to decide whether
{\mathcal{M}}\models^{{\mathtt{Diff}}}{\mathtt{RA}}
(similarly for
{\mathtt{WRA}}
and
{\mathtt{SRA}}
).

Lemma 7
.

\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Cons}}
is reducible to
\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Diff-Cons}}
(similarly for
{\mathtt{WRA}}
and
{\mathtt{SRA}}
).

The proof of this Lemma is given in Appendix
B
.

3
Algorithmic results

We are now ready to state our results for the
\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Cons}}
,

\operatorname{\mathtt{WRA-Cons}}
, and
\operatorname{\mathtt{SRA-Cons}}
problems. The results are stated in

Theorem

8
.
We prove that all the above problems are decidable. In the case of
{\mathtt{WRA}}
the complexity is polynomial to the size of the register machine, while for
{\mathtt{RA}}
and
{\mathtt{SRA}}
it is in
\mathsf{PSPACE}
and it is both
\mathsf{NP}
and
\mathsf{coNP}
-hard.
For
\operatorname{\mathtt{WRA-Cons}}
we prove it is sufficient to keep a constant amount of
information regarding paths in memory, and thus manage polynomial time complexity.
In the case of
\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Cons}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{SRA-Cons}}
the size of the data structures
increases, which implies an increment of the number amount of possible paths.
In these cases it is not sufficient to keep in memory only a constant number of information
for each paths we are exploring, but instead a polynomial one, which still yields a
\mathsf{PSPACE}
algorithm,
but the time complexity is no longer polynomial.

Theorem 8
.

For a given a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
of size
n
:

•

\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Cons}}
is in
{\mathsf{PSPACE}}
and it is both
\mathsf{NP}
and
\mathsf{coNP}
-hard.

•

\operatorname{\mathtt{SRA-Cons}}
is in
{\mathsf{PSPACE}}
and it is both
\mathsf{NP}
and
\mathsf{coNP}
-hard.

•

\operatorname{\mathtt{WRA-Cons}}
is in
\mathcal{O}([)n^{5}]
.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to describing the idea of our algorithm (s), the hardness results, and
proof sketches. The full proofs can be found in
Appendices
C
to
.

3.1
Algorithmic method for
\operatorname{\mathtt{WRA-Cons}}

Our
\mathsf{P}
-TIME algorithm for
{\mathtt{WRA}}
takes place in three modules.
All modules are based on a type of backwards reachability approach. The different modules start from potential violations of the condition they correspond to
and try to find paths leading to this violation by backtracking to the initial state.
We essentially keep track of what states have been
marked as origins of such potential violating paths, and on each iteration we process edges
leading to such states and propagate relevant information of existing paths backwards.

The main reason for choosing to have a backward search is that it makes dealing with
the copy operation easier.
We can apply the standard weakest pre-condition operator (see rule
\mathop{\hbox to11.37pt{\vbox to11.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.68657pt\lower-5.68657pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.59 0 C 7.59 4.19 4.19 7.59 0 7.59 C -4.19 7.59 -7.59 4.19 -7.59 0 C -7.59 -4.19 -4.19 -7.59 0 -7.59 C 4.19 -7.59 7.59 -4.19 7.59 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
in Fig.
5
) to maintain optimal complexity.
Having a forward search would lead to exponential branching over equivalence classes (where registers with identical values are kept in the same equivalence class).
Our modules concern the following correctness aspects for all runs of a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
:

•

{\mathcal{M}}
does not allow for
ghost reads
. Those are read events that can read the value of a register that has not been initialized yet. In the case that an implementation assumes initialized registers then this module
does not
need to be called.

•

{\mathcal{M}}
does not allow for
mismatched variable reads
.
Those are read events on a variable
x
, that read the value of a register that has been storing a value for a different variable
y
.

•

{\mathcal{M}}
does not allow for cyclic variable edit dependencies.
This last condition is the one that truly defines the
{\mathtt{WRA}}
semantics, as the other two are correctness conditions that are required from all memory models. This is seen in
.

The above conditions are all implied by the execution graph semantics stated in
Fig.

3
. However, in order to simplify our algorithm we check them separately.
In this way when we get to the most difficult condition of the above, which is the third, we do not need add extra checks for the previous ones.
For example, consider having encountered some
register
a
being used in some execution,
and we are storing some information about this,
we do not need to also keep track of which variable
x

was stored in
a
, since we have already confirmed that
there are no executions allowing for operations on other variables
to access registers that do not correspond to
x
.
We formalize the above statements as:

Proposition 9
.

Given a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}=\left\langle{Q,q_{\mathtt{init}},\Delta}\right\rangle
, we have that
{\mathcal{M}}\models{\mathtt{WRA}}
iff:

1.

For all
\rho\in{{\mathtt{Runs}}}\left({\mathcal{M}}\right)
,
\rho=\rho^{\prime}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta,x,a\right)}
, there exists an event
\mathtt{Events}
, such that
\rho=\rho_{0}\cdot\rho_{1}
, and
iseitheracopyorwriteeventthattargetsregister
a
.\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Forall
ρ
∈
Runs(
M)
,
ρ= ρ’
⋅
\mathsf{r}
,with
\mathsf{r}
=
(
R
,θ,x,a)
,thereexistsanevent
\mathsf{w}
_x
∈
Events
,suchthat
ρ= ρ_0
⋅
\mathsf{w}
_x
⋅
ρ_1
,and
\mathsf{w}
_x
writessomevalue
v
onregister
b
(possibly
b=a
),and
v
isthelastvalueassignedto
a
during
ρ_1
.\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Foreachrun
ρ
∈
Runs(
M)
,andfor
G_ρ =
mkEgraph(
ρ
)
=
⟨
E,
po
,
rf
,
co⟩
,

[
W
∧
\mathsf{w}
⋅
var]

⋅
hb
⋅
\mathsf{w}
⋅
hb
⋅
rf
^-1
isacyclicforeachwriteevent
\mathsf{w}
of
E
.\end{enumerate}\end{proposition}\par\begin{wrapfigure}[R][172.5pt]\begin{algorithm}{\hbox{\hbox{{{Input:}} }}${\mathcal{M}}$\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline}

3.2

Exposed Read Violations

Assume we are given a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}=\left\langle{Q,q_{\mathtt{init}},\Delta}\right\rangle
operating on a set
\theta
of threads, a set
{\mathcal{V}}
of variables, a set
\mathtt{Regs}
of registers, and a set
\mathtt{\mathcal{D}}
of data values.
We present an algorithm that checks whether all runs of
{\mathcal{M}}

respect the third condition of Proposition
9
.
Algorithm
assumes that
{\mathcal{M}}
has successfully passed the previous two stages of the verification (namely ghost reads and mismatched variables).

The key contributor to the complexity is the size of the
largest data structure necessary.
For
\operatorname{\mathtt{WRA-Cons}}
we need data structures of size

\mathcal{O}([)|Q|*|{\mathcal{V}}|*|\mathtt{Regs}|*|(\Theta\cup\mathtt{Regs})|]
,
which simplifies to
\mathcal{O}([)n^{4}]
.
These data structures are used to store summaries of possible runs
in
{\mathcal{M}}
.
We only keep information for runs that might cause violations
as the ones described
in Proposition
9
.
The addition of information (in the form of state-summary tuples) to our data structures is monotonic,
meaning once
we have discovered a path from some state
q
exists,
this information is never removed.
Thus the data structure size bounds the number of iterations
of our main loop (line
5
, Algorithm
).
For each iteration, the algorithm checks if an existing possible violation
path can be combined with more transitions of
{\mathcal{M}}
(a search which is linear
to the size of the machine), and creates more tuples to new states.
The conditions for guranteeing existence of new (relevant)
paths is characterized by the rules of Fig.
5
, which produce new
tuples (see lines
4
and
13
).
If no update takes place in one iteration, we are guaranteed we have arrived at a fixed-point
and the proceedure stops (lines
1
,
6
, and
14
).
Our data structures are:

The simple rules for
{\mathtt{WRA}}
. All rules apply only for
a\neq b
, and
x\neq y
.

Figure 5
:
The rules for updating the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{exposed}}}
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{fragile}}}
data structures.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{status}}}
means either
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{exposed}}}
or
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{fragile}}}
. These rules are sufficient to detect violations of
{\mathtt{WRA}}
, and for tracking
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
. When a rule is stated with an “or” description it stands for two rules, one for each version of this clause.

•

{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{fragile}}}}:{Q}\rightarrow{2^{{\mathcal{V}}\times(\Theta\cup\mathtt{Regs})\times\mathtt{Regs}}}
.
If
{q}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{fragile}}}}\left(a\right)\left(\theta\right)\left(x\right)\right]}\,{}

then there is a run
\rho
of
{\mathcal{M}}
starting at
q

in which
\theta
has a
{\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\right]}\,}^{+}

path to a read event

on

is in

(for example rule
\mathop{\hbox to11.43pt{\vbox to11.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.71465pt\lower-5.71465pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.63 0 C 7.63 4.21 4.21 7.63 0 7.63 C -4.21 7.63 -7.63 4.21 -7.63 0 C -7.63 -4.21 -4.21 -7.63 0 -7.63 C 4.21 -7.63 7.63 -4.21 7.63 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

in Fig.

A tuple
{q}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{fragile}}}}\left(a\right)\left(b\right)\left(x\right)\right]}\,{}

, which reads from the value of

on
\theta^{\prime}
(see rule
\mathop{\hbox to11.43pt{\vbox to11.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.71465pt\lower-5.71465pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.63 0 C 7.63 4.21 4.21 7.63 0 7.63 C -4.21 7.63 -7.63 4.21 -7.63 0 C -7.63 -4.21 -4.21 -7.63 0 -7.63 C 4.21 -7.63 7.63 -4.21 7.63 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

The tuple stands for an expectation of a tuple

(see rule
\mathop{\hbox to11.43pt{\vbox to11.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.71465pt\lower-5.71465pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.63 0 C 7.63 4.21 4.21 7.63 0 7.63 C -4.21 7.63 -7.63 4.21 -7.63 0 C -7.63 -4.21 -4.21 -7.63 0 -7.63 C 4.21 -7.63 7.63 -4.21 7.63 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

).

for a demonstration of this procedure.

•

{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{exposed}}}}:{Q}\rightarrow{2^{{\mathcal{V}}\times(\Theta\cup\mathtt{Regs})\times\mathtt{Regs}}}
.
These tuples are essentially an extension of the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{fragile}}}
tuples,
with the addition that the paths that were described above can now
refer to

{\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\right]}\,}^{+}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}_{x}{\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\right]}\,}^{+}

paths instead (for all
x\in{\mathcal{V}}
).

q_{1}
start
q_{2}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,a,\right)}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,b,\right)}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,y,c,\right)}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\phi,y,c\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\phi,x,a\right)}{}

(a)

Register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
.

e_{1}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,1\right)}
e_{2}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,2\right)}
e_{3}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,y,1\right)}
e_{4}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\phi,y,1\right)}
e_{5}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\phi,x,1\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}_{x}

(b)

A run
\rho
of
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Figure 6
:
A machine that produces an execution graph violating the
{\mathtt{WRA}}
semantics.

Proposition 10
.

For a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
,
{\mathcal{M}}\models{\mathtt{WRA}}
iff running
on
{\mathcal{M}}

flags no
{\tt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Unsafe}}
configurations.

The detailed proof of this statement are given in Appendix
E
.

Example 11
.

Consider the register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
shown in Fig.
6(a)
, and its run
\rho
shown in Fig.
6(b)
.
This execution graph does not respect
{\mathtt{WRA}}
, as reversing the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
edge from
e_{1}
to
e_{5}
, which means that the rules of
Fig.

5
should flag
{\tt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Unsafe}}
. We show the application of the rules for checking
{\mathtt{WRA}}
, as stated by
Proposition

10
.

The edge

This update now initializes a
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{exposed}}}
-type tuple.
This is because now not only we have confirmed the

{\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\right]}\,}^{+}

for

, which means that all other prior input events
that will take place on it, should be “hidden” from

.

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
, and produces the

{\tt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Unsafe}}
flag.

Example
11
only demonstrates the functionality of rules
\mathop{\hbox to8.56pt{\vbox to8.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.28227pt\lower-4.28227pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.65 0 C 5.65 3.12 3.12 5.65 0 5.65 C -3.12 5.65 -5.65 3.12 -5.65 0 C -5.65 -3.12 -3.12 -5.65 0 -5.65 C 3.12 -5.65 5.65 -3.12 5.65 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

, and
\mathop{\hbox to9.8pt{\vbox to9.8pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-4.89995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
.
The rest of the rules capture different ways of how paths can be propagataed to new states
in a register machine. Due to lack of space we cannot provide more
characteristic examples, but the exact function of each rule will be
demonstrated in the proof of correcness in Appendix
E
.

4

Upper and lower bounds for
\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Cons}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{SRA-Cons}}

In this section we show that both
\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Cons}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{SRA-Cons}}
are
\mathsf{coNP}
and
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.
The first reduction is from the problem of tautology to
\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Cons}}
. Namely, given a boolean formula
\varphi
over propositional variables
x,y,z,\dots
, we will construct, in polynomial time, a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
such that
{\mathcal{M}}\models{\mathtt{RA}}
if and only if
\varphi
is a tautology.
Without loss of generality we assume
\varphi
is in
3
-disjunctive normal form.

q_{x}
q_{x}^{F}
q_{x}^{T}
q_{x}^{\prime}
q_{y}
q_{y}^{F}
q_{y}^{T}
{q_{y}^{\prime}}
\epsilon
\epsilon
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a_{1}:=b_{1}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a_{2}:=b_{2}}
\epsilon
\epsilon
\epsilon
\epsilon
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a_{2}:=b_{2}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a_{1}:=b_{1}}
\epsilon
\epsilon
\epsilon

Figure 7
:
Assignment section of
{\mathcal{M}}
generated for a formula
\varphi=(z\wedge\overline{y})\vee(\overline{z}\wedge y)
. Note that
\varphi
is not in 3-DNF form, but it is sufficient as an explanatory example. Assigning true to
x
violates the second clause, so from
q_{x}^{T}
we copy the register
b_{2}
into register
a_{2}
.

•

Given the formula
\varphi
with
n
clauses, we parse it and create a register
machine over: Threads
\theta_{i}
with one thread per clause
c_{i}
of
\varphi
, a single variable
x
, and registers
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
corresponding to a clause
c_{i}
being satisfied or violated.

•

We construct three phases in the register machine:

3.1

The initialization of clauses, where the register machine performs
2
write events per clause
c_{i}
of the formula:

q_{2i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta_{i},x,b_{i-1\mod n},\right)}}q_{2i+1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta_{i},x,a_{i},\right)}}q_{2i+2}
,
starting at state
q_{0}
, and ending in state
q_{2n-1}
.
When this part of the register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
is executed
it always creates the same execution graph regardless of the actual
form of the clauses (see Fig.
in Appendix
).

3.2

The assignment phase, in which we construct,
for each variable
x
occurring in
\varphi
,
two states
q_{x}
and
q_{x}^{\prime}
, which we connect with two different paths.
The first of these paths start with an
\epsilon
-transition

q_{x}\to q_{x}^{T}
, which corresponds to assigning true to
x
.
Similarly, the second of these paths start with an

\epsilon
-transition
q_{x}\to q_{x}^{F}
,
which corresponds to assigning false to
x
.
Let
I\subseteq\left\{{0,\dots,n-1}\right\}
be the indices of clauses
in
\varphi
that contain
\overline{x}
. Each such clause is
unsatisfied by the assignment of true to
x
.
To mark this, we create a chain of copy transitions

a_{{i}}:=b_{{i}}
for each
i\in I
, starting from

q_{x}^{T}
and ending at
q_{x}^{\prime}
. Similarly,
let
J\subseteq\left\{{0,\dots,n-1}\right\}
be the indexes of clauses in

\varphi
that contain
x
, and create a chain of copy transitions

a_{{i}}:=b_{{i}}
for each
i\in J
,
starting from
q_{x}^{F}
and ending at
q_{x}^{\prime}
.
An example for the gadget for variables
x
and
y
can be seen in Fig.
7
.
For some arbitrary enumeration
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
of the variables of
\varphi
, we chain these assignment gadgets with
\epsilon
-transitions.

3.3

The final phase is the read phase, where the register machine performs
n
read transitions, one for each clause
c_{i}
:
q_{i}^{read}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta_{c_{i}},x,a_{{i}}\right)}}q_{i+1}^{read}

The three different phases of the machine are connected with
\epsilon
transitions for convenience.

The claim is that if there exists an assignment for the variables of
\varphi
which makes
\varphi
not hold then there is an
{\mathtt{SRA}}
violation of this register machine. The main idea here is that if there existed an assignment that violates the formula, this would imply that this assignment violates each clause in
\varphi
(remember that the formula is in 3DNF).
The full details are given in Appendix
. There we also give the modifications necessary for proving
\mathsf{NP}
hardness.

We note here that since
\operatorname{\mathtt{SRA-Cons}}
contains
\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Cons}}
problem (i.e., an algorithm solving the
\operatorname{\mathtt{SRA-Cons}}
would also detect the cycle we constructed above) these hardness results directly translate to the
{\mathtt{SRA}}
semantics.
Moreover, even though in the reduction we use
\epsilon
transitions and several copy instructions, neither of these is truly the cause of the complexity.
We can in fact create a similar construction, where we replace
\epsilon
transitions with writes on some irrelevant thread, and copy commands with carefully ordered overwrites of registers, and retain the same effect.
This implies that the cause of the
{\mathsf{coNP}}
and
{\mathsf{NP}}
hardness does not lie in this part of the expressiveness of the register machines.
However, it is likely that the lack of a better than
{\mathsf{PSPACE}}
algorithm is indeed caused by the copy commands (as one can see in the detailed algorithm below).

4.1

Membership in
\mathsf{PSPACE}

\theta
:
1
\ldots
2
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}

Figure 8
:
Short circuting a large cycle (red, dashed) in the execution graph of a
run, by following the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
edges of
\theta
.

Idea:

The key idea of our
\mathsf{PSPACE}
algorithm is that if the register machine

{\mathcal{M}}
can produce an arbitrarily long violating run
\rho
,
with arbitrarily many events in the corresponding execution graph

{{\mathsf{mkEgraph}}}\left(\rho\right)
, then it must also be able to produce a shorter
run
\rho_{s}
, which has at most length
2\times|\Theta|^{2}
, and
whose egraph contains the same cycle.
This is because if a cycle ”enters” the same thread
multiple times we are able to short-circuit the egraph cycle
by jumping directly from the first entry event (say
1
)
point to the event where the cycle exits that thread for the final time (say
2
)
without performing the intermediate steps and instead following
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}

edges, as seen in Fig.
8
. Thus we know that a minimal cycle for each thread enters each thread at most once and exits each thread at most once.
The algorithm uses non-deterministic guesses and works as follows:

•

First, it guesses which threads will be involved in a cycle, and
which transitions in the register machine correspond to
entering and leaving each one of the guessed threads.
The algorithm also guesses the type of edge that is connecting the guessed events in the corresponding execution graph cycle to each other.
Some of those edges in the egraph will be
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}
.
For the events that are connected via some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
we also guess linearly many extra events,
so we can guarantee the register machine would produce this
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}

dependency.

•

At this point we have reserved
n^{2}
-many events relevant for the cycle.
We also guess an order among these events, which we claim is the order in which they will occur in the register machine run

\rho
which will procude the cycle (if one exists).
It remains to determine whether the register
machine can indeed produce
\rho
. In what follows we will
store the explored part of the execution graph of the guessed
n^{2}
events
, along with the configuration of the registers.

•

We start running the register machine non-deterministically.
At each step we guess whether the next step is going to be
one of the reserved events for the cycle, or an event that
will change the configuration of the register machine
(as per the rules of Fig.
2
).

•

Since we only have
n^{2}
reserved events we know that these
non-deterministic checks are polynomially many.
However, the register machine might need to change exponentially many
configurations (pairs of state and register contents) before making
a specific value written during
\rho
available to the correct next
event of
\rho
(for example it might need to copy
it to different registers). In the worst case, we will need to explore all possible
register configurations between each reserved event.

•

If we manage to produce
\rho
before halting it means the input
{\mathcal{M}}
violates
the
{\mathtt{RA}}
(similarly for
{\mathtt{SRA}}
we keep track of the relevant cycles).

The above algorithm is an
\mathsf{NPSPACE}
algorithm, which however is enough to prove the
problem is in
{\mathsf{PSPACE}}
.
It is possible that in fact when checking whether a configuration of registers is
reachable from another one, the acutal complexity is simply non-deterministic
polynomial, something that would also drop the complexity of the above algorithm
to
{\mathsf{coNP}}
(which would be a tight bound).
However, we were not able to identify a formal argument on whether this is true or not.
We leave this problem as an avenue for future work.

5

Conclusion and Future Work

In this paper, we have taken the first steps towards a framework for verification under the RA semantics and its variants.
To that end, we prove a polynomial space upper bound (and polynomial time in the case of
\operatorname{\mathtt{WRA-Cons}}
) when the implementation is described in the classical register machine model.

Our first future endeavor is to close the complexity gap left by
Theorem
8
.
There, we were able to prove that the
\operatorname{\mathtt{RA-Cons}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{SRA-Cons}}
problems are both
\mathsf{NP}
- and
\mathsf{coNP}
-hard.
We are aware of the gap in complexity, and we did try to provide either a faster algorithm,
or a matching lower bound.
However, at the current state our results are already establishing the decidability, and our algorithms utilize
non-trivial ideas, and provide a platform for future improvements and approximations.
Moreover we already have a prototype tool implementation

[
6
]
of our the algorithms which would also heavily benefit from such progress.
We also plan to
leverage
abstraction and stateless model-checking techniques to achieve more efficient verification.

Aside from the above, we plan to consider more expressive modeling languages that make our applicable to a wider class of protocols at the cache, compiler, and application levels.
One such extension would be to consider data-dependent register machines, which could model for example
compare-and-swap
events.
This suggests that one would have to study a theory of equality/inequality to create an augmented register machine space and characterize all the possible executions.
In this case, we would expect a (much) higher complexity as we would need to work in the framework of well-structured systems
[
2
,
13
]
.
Another exciting extension to our register machine formalism would be to allow for transitions encoding more complex actions, such as
broadcast
,
rendezvous
, and
fences
.
We already know that these operations can be encoded with a series of transitions in the simple model, but having them explicitly as part of the syntax would create more succinct models and potentially speed up the verification.
Finally, another extension to the register machines would be in the direction of parameterized verification. We would be particularly interested in enhancing the model with the ability to handle arbitrarily many threads interacting with the memory without having to hard-code them as part of the register machine description.

Another direction of future work is to study the decidability and complexity of consistency checking for other memory models such as PSI(
[
25
]
), SC, TSO, and RC11.
A fundamental characteristic of our approach is using the declarative definition style for a consistency model.
We highlight for example the declarative definition of PSI (
[
22
, Definition 4]
), which requires the acyclicity of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}\cdot{\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}\right]}\,}^{+}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}}^{-1}
, i.e. only slightly more restrictive from the definition of
{\mathtt{SRA}}
(
Definition

3
).
Since the only relations necessary for this consistency model are already present in this work, this will be a natural and feasible next step.
On the other hand, SC, even though it is also defined declaratively (as an acyclicity condition of
{\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{{fr}}}}\right]}\,}^{+}
), is much harder to capture with our existing rules, as it uses a whole new type of relation, namely the
\mathsf{from-read}
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{{fr}}}}

[
4
]
) relation.
Moreover, the testing problem of whether a run
\rho
is SC is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, a bound that we expect will trivially also apply to the consistency checking problem since we can trivially create a register machine that generates only
\rho
.

Related Work
. In their seminal work
[
15
]
, Korach and Gibbons showed consistency testing under the SC semantics is NP-hard.
Alur et al. showed that the verification problem under the SC semantics
[
5
]
is undecidable, albeit for a data-dependent implementation model. Hence, one that registers machines cannot capture.

Several examples of protocols (
[
3
,
21
]
) are designed to enforce different consistency models.
Such works guarantee the designed mechanisms’ correctness and provide a good baseline for implementing practices.
However, they do not produce uniform frameworks or algorithms to answer the general consistency-checking question.

Bouajjani et al.
[
9
]
consider the verification problem for semantics, which is equivalent to
\mathtt{WRA}
, but with a model that allows unbounded numbers of pending messages.
They show the problem is
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-complete.

Another primary direction is verifying
single runs
, expressed as sequences of memory access events, to determine whether that run satisfies a consistency model.
This problem is quite complex in general, as, for at least all the memory models studied in this work, there is an unbounded number of possible reorderings of the observed events from one local view to the other.
Several works consider the testing problem under the
\mathtt{RA}
semantics
[
1
,
26
]
.
These works show that the testing problem has polynomial complexity for
\mathtt{RA}
.
Bouajjani et al.
[
9
]
show that the testing problem for the
\mathtt{WRA}
semantics has polynomial complexity.
Both works focus on providing a specific implementation or verifying a single run from an unknown implementation.

Appendix A

Reactive version of
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
from Fig.

Appendix B

Proofs of preliminary lemmata.

Proof of Lemma
6
.

Assume a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
and
\rho\in{{\mathtt{Runs}}}\left({\mathcal{M}}\right)
, and let
\left\langle\gamma_{\it init},G_{\emptyset}\right\rangle\xRightarrow{\rho}\left\langle\gamma_{1},G_{\rho}\right\rangle
, with
G_{\rho}=\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}\right\rangle
for some partial execution graph
G_{\rho}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}^{\prime}
be two write events on the same variable
x
, such that
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}^{\prime}),({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}})\not\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}_{x}
.
If either
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}^{\prime})
, or
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}})\in\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}_{x}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\right]}\,^{+}
, then we append the pair to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}
edge that respects the direction of this path and we are done.
Otherwise, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}^{\prime}
are incomparable w.r.t.
\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}_{x}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\right]}\,^{+}
then we pick such events that are locally maximal for
{\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}_{x}\right]}\,}^{+}
.
In this case, it is safe to pick at random one of
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}^{\prime}),({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}})
and add to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}_{x}
as no cycles will be introduced.
We continue the above process until we are left with a total
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}
for each variable
x
, and we are done.
The construction for
{\mathtt{WRA}}
and
{\mathtt{SRA}}
is identical, with the modification that we consider
\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\right]}\,^{+}
, and
\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{pco}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}}\right]}\,^{+}
paths, respectively.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7
.

We observe that the register machine model is inherently data independent (
[
9
]
,
[
27
]
).
We argue that if a non-differentiated run exists that violates the
{\mathtt{RA}}
, then so does a differentiated one.
To demonstrate this, for any arbitrary
{\mathcal{M}}
and
\rho\in{{\mathtt{Runs}}}\left({\mathcal{M}}\right)
, with
\left\langle\gamma_{\it init},G_{\emptyset}\right\rangle\xRightarrow{\rho}\left\langle\gamma_{1},G_{\rho}\right\rangle
, we assume
G_{\rho}\not\models{\mathtt{RA}}
.
Note that the transition
\xRightarrow{\rho}
is non-deterministic.
We now create a new run
\rho^{\prime}
, by defining a meta-counter of steps taken in
\rho
, and append that counter followed by a
\#
to all values written in the memory.
The new run is now differentiated, which means that
\xRightarrow{\rho^{\prime}}
is deterministic, and the register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
must take identical transitions while processing it (since it is data independent).
Thus we would get that the resulting execution graph is identical to
G_{\rho}
, and thus also would be a violating one.

In the converse direction, if a differentiated run violates a semantic model, the same run is a valid run of the register machine without the meta-variable modification.
Thus
{\mathcal{M}}\models{\mathtt{RA}}
iff
{\mathcal{M}}\models^{{\mathtt{Diff}}}{\mathtt{RA}}
(rsp.
{\mathtt{WRA}}
or
{\mathtt{SRA}}
), and we are done.
∎

The above can be similarly extended for
\operatorname{\mathtt{WRA-Cons}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{SRA-Cons}}
.

Proof of Proposition
9
.

Proof: Proposition
9
.

This proposition is counting on the fact that the run has been composed with the semantics of execution graphs, and thus all necessary updates for each event in
\rho
have taken place by the time the last event is outputted.
Assume
\rho\in{{\mathtt{Runs}}}\left({\mathcal{M}}\right)
, with
\rho=\rho^{\prime}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta,x,a\right)}
and since
{\mathcal{M}}\models{\mathtt{WRA}}
then
\rho\models{\mathtt{WRA}}
, and thus
\left\langle\gamma_{\it init},G_{\emptyset}\right\rangle\xRightarrow{\rho}\left\langle\gamma_{1},G\right\rangle
, with
G=\left\langle E,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{po}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{rf}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}\right\rangle
.

Questions

.
Clearly, as seen in
Fig.

, for this update to take place there exists event
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{w}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta,x,b,\mathtt{v}\right)}\in E
, with
\mathtt{v}
being the unique value (all runs are differentiated due to
Lemma

7
) that was outputted in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}
.
Assume now that the value
\mathtt{v}
was never written or copied on register

to perform the update
\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{\mathsf{r}}}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}

\mathtt{v}
would not be stored in

.
Therefore it must be the case that either the value of

has been copied to

\rho_{1}
, or that
a=b

\mathtt{Events}

Appendix D
Ghost reads and Mismatched-vars

D.1
Ghost reads

Here we give the algorithm and the proof of correctness
for detecting that for every read there is a corresponding write,
i.e. Question
1
from
9
.
We note that this particular question could easily be checked in polynomial time with a traditional breadth-first-search approach.
However, we chose to employ the backwards method that will be necessary later on from this stage already, so we can demonstrate the necessary concepts.
Our algorithm works as follows:

Figure 10
:
The inference rules for updating the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{regs}}}
data structure, for each state
q\in Q
of a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
. These rules constitute the implementation of the ghost-reads module. We assume that
b\neq a
.

•

It uses a data structure
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{regs}}}}:{Q}\rightarrow{2^{\mathtt{Regs}}}
.
If
\left\langle a\right\rangle\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{regs}}}(q)
then there is a run
\rho
of
{\mathcal{M}}
from
q
in which whatever was stored in
a
at
q
will be outputted.

•

It first isolates all output edges
E_{out}
of the register and then searches backwards through the edges or
{\mathcal{M}}
. We update the data structure as
Fig.

10
indicates.

•

If at any point a register
a
becomes relevant to the initial state
q_{init}
we associate the
{\tt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Unsafe}}
configuration to
q_{init}
. It follows that if while at
q_{init}
there exists a run where the register
a
value is will be output as it was in
q_{init}
, then it is possible to run the register machine and output from
a
without initialising it.

Intuitively,
Fig.

10
associates a register
a
to state
q
when there is a run from
q
outputting on some output edge the value of
a
exactly as it was on
q
.
These registers are propagated backwards to states
q^{\prime}
that can reach
q
depending on the edge that connects
q^{\prime}
to
q
.
A register is not propagated backwards to states
q^{\prime}
that can reach
q
if the transition leading to
q
is an input on
a
, as this means the value of
a
is now overwritten.
Upon termination this
\mathcal{O}([)n^{2}]
algorithm detects whether a register machine
{\mathcal{M}}
can output a ghost value.

q_{0}
start
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta_{1},x,a_{1},-\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta_{1},x,a_{2}\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta_{2},x,a_{1}\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta_{2},y,a_{2},-\right)}

Figure 11
:
A register machine that produces the pattern of
Fig.

1
. It satisfies
{\mathtt{WRA}}
, but not
{\mathtt{RA}}
, as there is a cycle of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{{co}}}}_{x}}}}}
edges.

Example 12
.

Consider the register machine of
Fig.

11
.
One can easily see that this register machine can produce a run

\rho=q_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{W}},\theta_{1},x,a_{1},-\right)}}q_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta_{2},x,a_{1}\right)}}q_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta_{1},x,a_{2}\right)}}q_{3}
, which outputs the value of
a_{2}
, even though nothing has been inputted on
a_{2}
during
\rho
.
Our algorithm from
Fig.

10
would first perform
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{regs}}}(q_{2})\oplus\left\langle a_{2}\right\rangle
, due to the

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{R}},\theta_{2},x,a_{2}\right)}
edge that would trigger rule
\mathop{\hbox to8.56pt{\vbox to8.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.28227pt\lower-4.28227pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.65 0 C 5.65 3.12 3.12 5.65 0 5.65 C -3.12 5.65 -5.65 3.12 -5.65 0 C -5.65 -3.12 -3.12 -5.65 0 -5.65 C 3.12 -5.65 5.65 -3.12 5.65 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.19444pt}{-2.57777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
, and then trigger rules

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and

in this order to perform the updates
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{regs}}}(q_{1})\oplus\left\langle a_{2}\right\rangle
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{regs}}}(q_{0})\oplus\left\langle a_{2}\right\rangle
respectively which would finally trigger rule
\mathop{\hbox to8.56pt{\vbox to8.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.28227pt\lower-4.28227pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.65 0 C 5.65 3.12 3.12 5.65 0 5.65 C -3.12 5.65 -5.65 3.12 -5.65 0 C -5.65 -3.12 -3.12 -5.65 0 -5.65 C 3.12 -5.65 5.65 -3.12 5.65 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
